target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;MCP&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; is an open standard that enables developers to build a two-way connection between AI applications and the data sources they need to access. In this architecture, the MCP client is the integration point with the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/ai/what-is-large-language-model/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;LLM&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; or other &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/ai/what-is-agentic-ai/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;AI agent&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and the MCP server sits between the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/ai/mcp-client-and-server/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;MCP client&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and the corporate resources.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/73kpNxOQIlM0UOnty9qGXS/53fe1b92e299b52363ac1870df52f2e9/BLOG-3252_2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3252 2\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;979\&quot; height=\&quot;401\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;The separation between MCP clients and MCP servers allows agents to autonomously pursue goals and take actions while maintaining a clear boundary between the AI (integrated at the MCP client) and the credentials and APIs of the corporate resource (integrated at the MCP server).Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Our workforce at Cloudflare is constantly using MCP servers to access information in various internal resources, including our project management platform, our internal wiki, documentation and code management platforms, and more.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Very early on, we realized that locally-hosted MCP servers were a security liability. Local MCP server deployments may rely on unvetted software sources and versions, which increases the risk of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://owasp.org/www-project-mcp-top-10/2025/MCP04-2025%E2%80%93Software-Supply-Chain-Attacks&amp;amp;Dependency-Tampering\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;supply chain attacks&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href=\&quot;https://owasp.org/www-community/attacks/MCP_Tool_Poisoning\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;tool injection attacks&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. They prevent IT and security administrators from administrating these servers, leaving it up to individual employees and developers to choose which MCP servers they want to run and how they want to keep them up to date. This is a losing game.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4mngDeTGrsah2DiN7IAnrf/575075096e72d6b967df4327a99c35fc/BLOG-3252_3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3252 3\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;384\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Instead, we have a centralized team at Cloudflare that manages our MCP server deployment across the enterprise. This team built a shared MCP platform inside our monorepo that provides governed infrastructure out of the box. When an employee wants to expose an internal resource via MCP, they first get approval from our AI governance team, and then they copy a template, write their tool definitions, and deploy, all the while inheriting default-deny write controls with audit logging, auto-generated &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/serverless/glossary/what-is-ci-cd/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;CI/CD pipelines&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/security/glossary/secrets-management/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;secrets management&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for free. This means standing up a new governed MCP server is minutes of scaffolding. The governance is baked into the platform itself, which is what allowed adoption to spread so quickly.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Our CI/CD pipeline deploys them as &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/agents/guides/remote-mcp-server/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;remote MCP servers&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; on custom domains on &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/developer-platform/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflareâs developer platform&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. This gives us visibility into which MCPs servers are being used by our employees, while maintaining control over software sources. As an added bonus, every remote MCP server on the Cloudflare developer platform is automatically deployed across our global network of data centers, so MCP servers can be accessed by our employees with low latency, regardless of where they might be in the world.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;cloudflare-access-provides-authentication\&quot;&gt;Cloudflare Access provides authentication&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#cloudflare-access-provides-authentication\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Some of our MCP servers sit in front of public resources, like our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://docs.mcp.cloudflare.com/mcp\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare documentation MCP server&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.mcp.cloudflare.com/mcp\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Radar MCP server&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and thus we want them to be accessible to anyone. But many of the MCP servers used by our workforce are sitting in front of our private corporate resources. These MCP servers require user authentication to ensure that they are off limits to everyone but authorized Cloudflare employees. To achieve this, our monorepo template for MCP servers integrates &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/sase/products/access/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Access&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; as the OAuth provider. Cloudflare Access secures login flows and issues access tokens to resources, while acting as an identity aggregator that verifies end user &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/access-management/what-is-sso/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;single-sign on (SSO)&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/access-management/what-is-multi-factor-authentication/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;multifactor authentication (MFA)&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and a variety of contextual attributes such as IP addresses, location, or device certificates.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;mcp-server-portals-centralize-discovery-and-governance\&quot;&gt;MCP server portals centralize discovery and governance&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#mcp-server-portals-centralize-discovery-and-governance\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    \n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/70s5yIwdzoaYQIoRF4L0mH/018dae32529c30639a2af48ee4031bc6/BLOG-3252_4.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3252 4\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1200\&quot; height=\&quot;680\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;MCP server portals unify governance and control for all AI activity.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;As the number of our remote MCP servers grew, we hit a new wall: discovery. We wanted to make it easy for every employee (especially those that are new to MCP) to find and work with all the MCP servers that are available to them. Our MCP server portals product provided a convenient solution. The employee simply connects their MCP client to the MCP server portal, and the portal immediately reveals every internal and third-party MCP servers they are authorized to use.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Beyond this, our MCP server portals provide centralized logging, consistent policy enforcement and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/access-management/what-is-dlp/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;data loss prevention&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (DLP guardrails). Our administrators can see who logged into what MCP portal and create DLP rules that prevent certain data, like personally identifiable data (PII), from being shared with certain MCP servers.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We can also create policies that control who has access to the portal itself, and what tools from each MCP server should be exposed. For example, we could set up one MCP server portal that is only accessible to employees that are part of our &lt;i&gt;finance &lt;/i&gt;group that exposes just the read-only tools for the MCP server in front of our internal code repository. Meanwhile, a different MCP server portal, accessible only to employees on their corporate laptops that are in our &lt;i&gt;engineering &lt;/i&gt;team, could expose more powerful read/write tools to our code repository MCP server.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;An overview of our MCP server portal architecture is shown above. The portal supports both remote MCP servers hosted on Cloudflare, and third-party MCP servers hosted anywhere else. What makes this architecture uniquely performant is that all these security and networking components run on the same physical machine within our global network. When an employee&amp;#39;s request moves through the MCP server portal, a Cloudflare-hosted remote MCP server, and Cloudflare Access, their traffic never needs to leave the same physical machine.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;code-mode-with-mcp-server-portals-reduces-costs\&quot;&gt;Code Mode with MCP server portals reduces costs&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#code-mode-with-mcp-server-portals-reduces-costs\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;After months of high-volume MCP deployments, weâve paid out our fair share of tokens. Weâve also started to think most people are doing MCP wrong.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The standard approach to MCP requires defining a separate tool for every API operation that is exposed via an MCP server. But this static and exhaustive approach quickly exhausts an agentâs context window, especially for large platforms with thousands of endpoints.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We previously wrote about how we used server-side &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/code-mode-mcp/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Code Mode to power Cloudflareâs MCP server&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, allowing us to expose &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/api/?cf_target_id=C3927C0A6A2E9B823D2DF3F28E5F0D30\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;the thousands of end-points in Cloudflare API&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; while reducing token use by 99.9%. The Cloudflare MCP server exposes just two tools: a &lt;code&gt;search&lt;/code&gt; tool lets the model write JavaScript to explore whatâs available, and an &lt;code&gt;execute&lt;/code&gt; tool lets it write JavaScript to call the tools it finds. The model discovers what it needs on demand, rather than receiving everything upfront.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We like this pattern so much, we had to make it available for everyone. So we have now launched the ability to use the âCode Modeâ pattern with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/access-controls/ai-controls/mcp-portals/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;MCP server portals&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Now you can front all of your MCP servers with a centralized portal that performs audit controls and progressive tool disclosure, in order to reduce token costs.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Here is how it works. Instead of exposing every tool definition to a client, all of your underlying MCP servers collapse into just two MCP portal tools: &lt;code&gt;portal_codemode_search&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;portal_codemode_execute&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;code&gt;search&lt;/code&gt; tool gives the model access to a &lt;code&gt;codemode.tools()&lt;/code&gt; function that returns all the tool definitions from every connected upstream MCP server. The model then writes JavaScript to filter and explore these definitions, finding exactly the tools it needs without every schema being loaded into context. The &lt;code&gt;execute&lt;/code&gt; tool provides a &lt;code&gt;codemode&lt;/code&gt; proxy object where each upstream tool is available as a callable function. The model writes JavaScript that calls these tools directly, chaining multiple operations, filtering results, and handling errors in code. All of this runs in a sandboxed environment on the MCP server portal powered by &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/dynamic-workers/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Dynamic Workers&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Here is an example of an agent that needs to find a Jira ticket and update it with information from Google Drive. It first searches for the right tools:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-javascript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-javascript\&quot;&gt;// portal_codemode_search\nasync () =&amp;gt; {\n const tools = await codemode.tools();\n return tools\n  .filter(t =&amp;gt; t.name.includes(&amp;quot;jira&amp;quot;) || t.name.includes(&amp;quot;drive&amp;quot;))\n  .map(t =&amp;gt; ({ name: t.name, params: Object.keys(t.inputSchema.properties || {}) }));\n}\n&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt; The model now knows the exact tool names and parameters it needs, without the full schemas of tools ever entering its context. It then writes a single &lt;code&gt;execute&lt;/code&gt; call to chain the operations together:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-javascript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-javascript\&quot;&gt;// portal_codemode_execute\nasync () =&amp;gt; {\n const tickets = await codemode.jira_search_jira_with_jql({\n  jql: âproject = BLOG AND status = âIn Progressââ,\n  fields: [âsummaryâ, âdescriptionâ]\n });\n const doc = await codemode.google_workspace_drive_get_content({\n  fileId: â1aBcDeFgHiJkâ\n });\n await codemode.jira_update_jira_ticket({\n  issueKey: tickets[0].key,\n  fields: { description: tickets[0].description + â\\n\\nâ + doc.content }\n });\n return { updated: tickets[0].key };\n}\n&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;This is just two tool calls. The first discovers what&amp;#39;s available, the second does the work. Without Code Mode, this same workflow would have required the model to receive the full schemas of every tool from both MCP servers upfront, and then make three separate tool invocations.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Letâs put the savings in perspective: when our internal MCP server portal is connected to just four of our internal MCP servers, it exposes 52 tools that consume approximately 9,400 tokens of context just for their definitions. With Code Mode enabled, those 52 tools collapse into 2 portal tools consuming roughly 600 tokens, a 94% reduction. And critically, this cost stays fixed. As we connect more MCP servers to the portal, the token cost of Code Mode doesnât grow.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Code Mode can be activated on an MCP server portal by adding a query parameter to the URL. Instead of connecting to your portal over its usual URL (e.g. &lt;code&gt;https://myportal.example.com/mcp&lt;/code&gt;), you attach &lt;code&gt;?codemode=search_and_execute&lt;/code&gt; to the URL (e.g. &lt;code&gt;https://myportal.example.com/mcp?codemode=search_and_execute&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;ai-gateway-provides-extensibility-and-cost-controls\&quot;&gt;AI Gateway provides extensibility and cost controls&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#ai-gateway-provides-extensibility-and-cost-controls\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We arenât done yet. We plug &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/developer-platform/products/ai-gateway/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;AI Gateway&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; into our architecture by positioning it on the connection between the MCP client and the LLM. This allows us to quickly switch between various LLM providers (to prevent vendor lock-in) and to enforce cost controls (by limiting the number of tokens each employee can burn through). The full architecture is shown below. &lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3epLuGydMO1YxkdhkcqGPG/74c26deb712d383942e79699b4fb71da/BLOG-3252_5.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3252 5\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1200\&quot; height=\&quot;680\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;cloudflare-gateway-discovers-and-blocks-shadow-mcp\&quot;&gt;Cloudflare Gateway discovers and blocks shadow MCP&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#cloudflare-gateway-discovers-and-blocks-shadow-mcp\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Now that weâve provided governed access to authorized MCP servers, letâs look into dealing with unauthorized MCP servers. We can perform shadow MCP discovery using &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-wan/zero-trust/cloudflare-gateway/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Gateway&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Cloudflare Gateway is our comprehensive secure web gateway that provides enterprise security teams with visibility and control over their employeesâ Internet traffic.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We can use the Cloudflare Gateway API to perform a multi-layer scan to find remote MCP servers that are not being accessed via an MCP server portal. This is possible using a variety of existing Gateway and Data Loss Prevention (DLP) selectors, including:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Using the Gateway &lt;code&gt;httpHost&lt;/code&gt; selector to scan forÂ &lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;known MCP server hostnames using (like &lt;a href=\&quot;http://mcp.stripe.com\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;mcp.stripe.com&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;)&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;mcp.* subdomains using wildcard hostname patternsÂ &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Using the Gateway &lt;code&gt;httpRequestURI&lt;/code&gt; selector to scan for MCP-specific URL paths like /mcp and /mcp/sseÂ &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Using DLP-based body inspection to find MCP traffic, even if that traffic uses URI that do not contain the telltale mentions of &lt;code&gt;mcp&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;sse&lt;/code&gt;. Specifically, we use the fact that MCP uses JSON-RPC over HTTP, which means every request contains a &amp;quot;method&amp;quot; field with values like &amp;quot;tools/call&amp;quot;, &amp;quot;prompts/get&amp;quot;, or &amp;quot;initialize.&amp;quot; Here are some regex rules that can be used to detect MCP traffic in the HTTP body:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-javascript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-javascript\&quot;&gt;const DLP_REGEX_PATTERNS = [\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Initialize Method&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;initialize&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Tools Call&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;tools/call&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Tools List&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;tools/list&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Resources Read&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;resources/read&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Resources List&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;resources/list&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Prompts List&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;prompts/(list|get)&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Sampling Create Message&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;sampling/createMessage&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Protocol Version&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;protocolVersion&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;202[4-9]&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Notifications Initialized&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;notifications/initialized&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n  {\n    name: &amp;quot;MCP Roots List&amp;quot;,\n    regex: &amp;#039;&amp;quot;method&amp;quot;\\\\s{0,5}:\\\\s{0,5}&amp;quot;roots/list&amp;quot;&amp;#039;,\n  },\n];\n&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;The Gateway API supports additional automation. For example, one can use the custom DLP profile we defined above to block traffic, or redirect it, or just to log and inspect MCP payloads. Put this together, and Gateway can be used to provide comprehensive detection of unauthorized remote MCP servers accessed via an enterprise network.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For more information on how to build this out, see this &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/tutorials/detect-mcp-traffic-gateway-logs/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;tutorial&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;public-facing-mcp-servers-are-protected-with-ai-security-for-apps\&quot;&gt;Public-facing MCP Servers are protected with AI Security for Apps&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#public-facing-mcp-servers-are-protected-with-ai-security-for-apps\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;So far, weâve been focused on protecting our workforceâs access to our internal MCP servers. But, like many other organizations, we also have public-facing MCP servers that our customers can use to agentically administer and operate Cloudflare products. These MCP servers are hosted on Cloudflareâs developer platform. (You can find a list of individual MCPs for specific products &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/agents/model-context-protocol/mcp-servers-for-cloudflare/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;here&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, or refer back to our new approach for providing more efficient access to the entire Cloudflare API using &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/code-mode/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Code Mode&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.)&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We believe that every organization should publish official, first-party MCP servers for their products. The alternative is that your customers source unvetted servers from public repositories where packages may contain &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.docker.com/blog/mcp-horror-stories-the-supply-chain-attack/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;dangerous trust assumptions&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, undisclosed data collection, and any range of unsanctioned behaviors. By publishing your own MCP servers, you control the code, update cadence, and security posture of the tools your customers use.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Since every remote MCP server is an HTTP endpoint, we can put it behind the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/application-services/products/waf/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Web Application Firewall (WAF)&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Customers can enable the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/waf/detections/ai-security-for-apps/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;AI Security for Apps&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; feature within the WAF to automatically inspect inbound MCP traffic for prompt injection attempts, sensitive data leakage, and topic classification. Public facing MCPs are protected just as any other web API.Â Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-future-of-mcp-in-the-enterprise\&quot;&gt;The future of MCP in the enterprise&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-future-of-mcp-in-the-enterprise\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We hope our experience, products, and reference architectures will be useful to other organizations as they continue along their own journey towards broad enterprise-wide adoption of MCP.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Weâve secured our own MCP workflows by:Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Offering our developers a templated framework for building and deploying remote MCP servers on our developer platform using Cloudflare Access for authentication&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Ensuring secure, identity-based access to authorized MCP servers by connecting our entire workforce to MCP server portals&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Controlling costs using AI Gateway to mediate access to the LLMs powering our workforceâs MCP clients, and using Code Mode in MCP server portals to reduce token consumption and context bloat&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/tutorials/detect-mcp-traffic-gateway-logs/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Discovering&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; shadow MCP usage by Cloudflare GatewayÂ &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;For organizations advancing on their own enterprise MCP journeys, we recommend starting by putting your existing remote and third-party MCP servers behind &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/access-controls/ai-controls/mcp-portals/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Â Cloudflare MCP server portals&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and enabling Code Mode to start benefitting for cheaper, safer and simpler enterprise deployments of MCP.Â Â  &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;i&gt;Acknowledgements:Â  This reference architecture and blog represents this work of many people across many different roles and business units at Cloudflare. This is just a partial list of contributors: Ann Ming Samborski,Â  Kate Reznykova, Mike Nomitch,Â James Royal, Liam Reese, Yumna Moazzam, Simon Thorpe, Rian van der Merwe, Rajesh Bhatia, Ayush Thakur, By adopting RFC 9728, agents can authenticate on behalf of users without using insecure service accounts.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;We have thousands of internal apps at Cloudflare. Some are things weâve built ourselves, others are self-hosted instances of software built by others. They range from business-critical apps nearly every person uses, to side projects and prototypes.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;All of these apps are protected by &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/access-controls/policies/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Access&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. But when we started using and building agents âÂ particularly for uses beyond writing code â we hit a wall. People could access apps behind Access, but their agents couldnât.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Access sits in front of internal apps. You define a policy, and then Access will send unauthenticated users to a login page to choose how to authenticate.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3867rU5n4IkRilnfCAvOLD/8ec5ea3c25dbd6620a8644d231893732/BLOG-3146_2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3146 2\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1633\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Example of a Cloudflare Access login page&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This flow worked great for humans. But all agents could see was a redirect to a login page that they couldnât act on.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Providing agents with access to internal app data is so vital that we immediately implemented a stopgap for our own internal use. We modified OpenCodeâs &lt;a href=\&quot;https://opencode.ai/docs/tools/#webfetch\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;web fetch tool&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; such that for specific domains, it triggered the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/tutorials/cli/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;cloudflared&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; CLI to open an authorization flow to fetch a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/access-management/token-based-authentication/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;JWT&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (JSON Web Token). By appending this token to requests, we enabled secure, immediate access to our internal ecosystem.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;While this solution was a temporary answer to our own dilemma, today weâre retiring this workaround and fixing this problem for everyone. Now in open beta, every Access application supports managed OAuth. One click to enable it for an Access app, and agents that speak OAuth 2.0 can easily discover how to authenticate (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc9728\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;RFC 9728&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;), send the user through the auth flow, and receive back an authorization token (the same JWT from our initial solution).Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Now, the flow works smoothly for both humans and agents. Cloudflare Access has a generous &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/plans/zero-trust-services/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;free tier&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. And building off our newly-introduced &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/organizations-beta/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Organizations beta&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, youâll soon be able to bridge identity providers across Cloudflare accounts too.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;how-managed-oauth-works\&quot;&gt;How managed OAuth works&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#how-managed-oauth-works\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;For a given internal app protected by Cloudflare Access, you enable managed OAuth in one click:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/79FSFNeaDbqn9DkSyfKrtz/5f0fd06ce9e127c06474263a529ec284/BLOG-3146_3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3146 3\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1426\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Once managed OAuth is enabled, Cloudflare Access acts as the authorization server. It returns the &lt;code&gt;www-authenticate&lt;/code&gt; header, telling unauthorized agents where to look up information on how to get an authorization token. They find this at &lt;code&gt;https://&amp;lt;your-app-domain&amp;gt;/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server&lt;/code&gt;. Equipped with that direction, agents can just follow OAuth standards:Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The agent dynamically registers itself as a client (a process known as Dynamic Client Registration âÂ &lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7591\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;RFC 7591&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;),Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The agent sends the human through a PKCE (Proof Key for Code Exchange) authorization flow (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7636\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;RFC 7636&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;)&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The human authorizes access, which grants a token to the agent that it can use to make authenticated requests on behalf of the user&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hereâs what the authorization flow looks like:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/2LkJMuPCNX1Mu7MfEnFinH/fef5cf3f87e83d93c49ffeadbe6aed4d/BLOG-3146_4.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3146 4\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1406\&quot; height=\&quot;1999\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;If this authorization flow looks familiar, thatâs because itâs what the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; uses. We originally built support for this into our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/access-controls/ai-controls/mcp-portals/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;MCP server portals&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; product, which proxies and controls access to many MCP servers, to allow the portal to act as the OAuth server. Now, weâre bringing this to all Access apps so agents can access not only MCP servers that require authorization, but also web pages, web apps, and REST APIs.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;mass-upgrading-your-internal-apps-to-be-agent-ready\&quot;&gt;Mass upgrading your internal apps to be agent-ready&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#mass-upgrading-your-internal-apps-to-be-agent-ready\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Upgrading the long tail of internal software to work with agents is a daunting task. In principle, in order to be agent-ready, every internal and external app would ideally have discoverable APIs, a CLI, a well-crafted MCP server, and have adopted the many emerging agent standards.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;AI adoption is not something that can wait for everything to be retrofitted. Most organizations have a significant backlog of apps built over many years. And many internal âappsâ work great when treated by agents as simple websites. For something like an internal wiki, all you really need is to enable &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/markdown-for-agents/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Markdown for Agents&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, turn on managed OAuth, and agents have what they need to read protected content.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To make the basics work across the widest set of internal applications, we use Managed OAuth. By putting Access in front of your legacy internal apps, you make them agent-ready instantly. No code changes, no retrofitting. Instead, just immediate compatibility.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;its-the-users-agent-no-service-accounts-and-tokens-needed\&quot;&gt;Itâs the userâs agent. No service accounts and tokens needed&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#its-the-users-agent-no-service-accounts-and-tokens-needed\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Agents need to act on behalf of users inside organizations. One of the biggest anti-patterns weâve seen is people provisioning service accounts for their agents and MCP servers, authenticated using static credentials. These have their place in simple use cases and quick prototypes, and Cloudflare Access supports &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/access-controls/service-credentials/service-tokens/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;service tokens&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for this purpose.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But the service account approach quickly shows its limits when fine-grained access controls and audit logs are required. We believe that every action an agent performs must be easily attributable to the human who initiated it, and that an agent must only be able to perform actions that its human operator is likewise authorized to do. Service accounts and static credentials become points at which attribution is lost. Agents that launder all of their actions through a service account are susceptible to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confused_deputy_problem\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;confused deputy problems&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and result in audit logs that appear to originate from the agent itself.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For security and accountability, agents must use security primitives capable of expressing this userâagent relationship. OAuth is the industry standard protocol for requesting and delegating access to third parties. It gives agents a way to talk to your APIs on behalf of the user, with a token scoped to the userâs identity, so that access controls correctly apply and audit logs correctly attribute actions to the end user.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;standards-for-the-win-how-agents-can-and-should-adopt-rfc-9728-in-their-web-fetch-tools\&quot;&gt;Standards for the win: how agents can and should adoptÂ RFC 9728 in their web fetch tools&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#standards-for-the-win-how-agents-can-and-should-adopt-rfc-9728-in-their-web-fetch-tools\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc9728\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;RFC 9728&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; is the OAuth standard that makes it possible for agents to discover where and how to authenticate. It standardizes where this information lives and how itâs structured. This RFC became official in April 2025 and was quickly adopted by the Model Context Protocol (MCP), which now &lt;a href=\&quot;https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2025-11-25/basic/authorization\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;requires that both MCP servers and clients support it&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But outside of MCP, agents should adopt RFC 9728 for an even more essential use case: making requests to web pages that are protected behind OAuth and making requests to plain old REST APIs.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Most agents have a tool for making basic HTTP requests to web pages. This is commonly called the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/web-fetch-tool\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;âweb fetchâ tool&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Itâs similar to using the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Fetch_API\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;fetch()&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; API in JavaScript, often with some additional post-processing on the response. Itâs what lets you paste a URL into your agent and have your agent go look up the content.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Today, most agentsâ web fetch tools wonât do anything with the &lt;code&gt;www-authenticate&lt;/code&gt; header that a URL returns. The underlying model might choose to introspect the response headers and figure this out on its own, but the tool itself does not follow &lt;code&gt;www-authenticate&lt;/code&gt;, look up &lt;code&gt;/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server&lt;/code&gt;, and act as the client in the OAuth flow. But it &lt;i&gt;can&lt;/i&gt;, and we strongly believe it &lt;i&gt;should&lt;/i&gt;! Agents already do this to act as remote MCP clients.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To demonstrate this, weâve put up a draft pull request that &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/anomalyco/opencode/pull/22096/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;adapts the web fetch tool in Opencode&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to show this in action. Before making a request, the adapted tool first checks whether it already has credentials ; if it does, it uses them to make the initial request. If the tool gets back a 401 or a 403 with a &lt;code&gt;www-authenticate&lt;/code&gt; header, it asks the user for consent to be sent through the serverâs OAuth flow.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hereâs how that OAuth flow works. If you give the agent a URL that is protected by OAuth and complies with RFC 9728, the agent prompts the human for consent to open the authorization flow:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/1vo4hGtVz7ovlNQslTvTal/3d2b1a861b9342842bd6297e1869aed4/BLOG-3146_5.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3146 5\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1488\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;â¦sending the human to the login page:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3867rU5n4IkRilnfCAvOLD/8ec5ea3c25dbd6620a8644d231893732/BLOG-3146_2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3146 2\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1633\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;â¦and then to a consent dialog that prompts the human to grant access to the agent:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/39I0yjWXKGLNG4mhV5jEPQ/793559a5f18a04e807939b9560118ccb/BLOG-3146_6.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3146 6\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1818\&quot; height=\&quot;1644\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Once the human grants access to the agent, the agent uses the token it has received to make an authenticated request:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4e0mNkih5xtbAoQpwBrB32/701089e8ea256804890d45af08ef04ca/BLOG-3146_7.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3146 7\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1796\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Any agent from Codex to Claude Code to Goose and beyond can implement this, and thereâs nothing bespoke to Cloudflare. Itâs all built using OAuth standards.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We think this flow is powerful, and that supporting RFC 9728 can help agents with more than just making basic web fetch requests. If a REST API supports RFC 9728 (and the agent does too), the agent has everything it needs to start making authenticated requests against that API. If the REST API supports &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9727\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;RFC 9727&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, then the client can discover a catalog of REST API endpoints on its own, and do even more without additional documentation, agent skills, MCP servers or CLIs.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Each of these play important roles with agents â Cloudflare itself provides an &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/code-mode-mcp/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;MCP server for the Cloudflare API&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (built using &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/code-mode/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Code Mode&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;), &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/wrangler/commands/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Wrangler CLI&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/cloudflare/skills\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Agent Skills&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://x.com/CloudflareDev/status/2037336582815175122\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Plugin&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. But supporting RFC 9728 helps ensure that even when none of these are preinstalled, agents have a clear path forward. If the agent has a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/dynamic-workers/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;sandbox to execute untrusted code&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;,Â it can just write and execute code that calls the API that the human has granted it access to. Weâre working on supporting this for Cloudflareâs own APIs, to help your agents understand how to use Cloudflare.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;coming-soon-share-one-identity-provider-idp-across-many-cloudflare-accounts\&quot;&gt;Coming soon: share one identity provider (IdP) across many Cloudflare accounts&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#coming-soon-share-one-identity-provider-idp-across-many-cloudflare-accounts\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;At Cloudflare our own internal apps are deployed to dozens of different Cloudflare accounts, which are all part of an Organization â a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/organizations-beta/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;newly introduced&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; way for administrators to manage users, configurations, and view analytics across many Cloudflare accounts. We have had the same challenge as many of our customers: each Cloudflare account has to separately configure an IdP, so Cloudflare Access uses our identity provider. Itâs critical that this is consistent across an organization â you donât want one Cloudflare account to inadvertently allow people to sign in just with a one-time PIN, rather than requiring that they authenticate via single-sign on (SSO).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To solve this, weâre currently working on making it possible to share an identity provider across Cloudflare accounts, giving organizations a way to designate a single primary IdP for use across every account in their organization.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;As new Cloudflare accounts are created within an organization, administrators will be able to configure a bridge to the primary IdP with a single click, so Access applications across accounts can be protected by one identity provider. This removes the need to manually configure IdPs account by account, which is a process that doesnât scale for organizations with many teams and individuals each operating their own accounts.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;whats-next\&quot;&gt;Whatâs next&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#whats-next\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Across companies, people in every role and business function are now using agents to build internal apps, and expect their agents to be able to access context from internal apps. By combining graph neural networks and LLMs, we&#39;ve reduced false positives by up to 200x while catching sophisticated zero-day exploits.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Client-side skimming attacks have a boring superpower: they can steal data without breaking anything. The page still loads. Checkout still completes. All it needs is just one malicious script tag.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;If that sounds abstract, here are two recent examples of such skimming attacks:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;In January 2026, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://sansec.io/research/keylogger-major-us-bank-employees\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Sansec reported&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; a browser-side keylogger running on an employee merchandise store for a major U.S. bank, harvesting personal data, login credentials, and credit card information.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;In September 2025, attackers published malicious releases of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-cloudflares-client-side-security-made-the-npm-supply-chain-attack-a-non/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;widely used npm packages&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. If those packages were bundled into front-end code, end users could be exposed to crypto-stealing in the browser.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;To further our goal of building a better Internet, Cloudflare established a core tenet during our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/innovation-week/birthday-week-2025/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Birthday Week 2025&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;: powerful security features should be accessible &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/enterprise-grade-features-for-all/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;without requiring a sales engagement&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. In pursuit of this objective, we are announcing two key changes today:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;First, Cloudflare &lt;b&gt;Client-Side Security Advanced&lt;/b&gt; (formerly &lt;b&gt;Page Shield add-on&lt;/b&gt;) is now &lt;a href=\&quot;https://dash.cloudflare.com/?to=/:account/:zone/security/settings?tabs=client-side-abuse\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;available to self-serve&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; customers. And second, domain-based threat intelligence is now complimentary for all customers on the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/page-shield/#availability\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;free &lt;/u&gt;&lt;b&gt;&lt;u&gt;Client-Side Security&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/b&gt;&lt;u&gt; bundle&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In this post, weâll explain how this product works and highlight a new AI detection system designed to identify malicious JavaScript while minimizing false alarms. Weâll also discuss several real-world applications for these tools.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;how-cloudflare-client-side-security-works\&quot;&gt;How Cloudflare Client-Side Security works&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#how-cloudflare-client-side-security-works\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Cloudflare Client-Side Security assesses 3.5 billion scripts per day, protecting 2,200 scripts per enterprise zone on average.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Under the hood, Client-Side Security collects these signals using browser reporting (for example, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Guides/CSP\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Content Security Policy&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;), which means you donât need scanners or app instrumentation to get started, and there is zero latency impact to your web applications. The only prerequisite is that your traffic is proxied through Cloudflare.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Client-Side Security &lt;b&gt;Advanced&lt;/b&gt; provides immediate access to powerful security features:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Smarter malicious script detection:&lt;/b&gt; Using in-house machine learning, this capability is now enhanced with assessments from a Large Language Model (LLM). Read more details below.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Code change monitoring:&lt;/b&gt; Continuous code change detection and monitoring is included, which is essential for meeting compliance like&lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/page-shield/reference/pci-dss/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt; &lt;u&gt;PCI DSS v4&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, requirement 11.6.1.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Proactive blocking rules:&lt;/b&gt; Benefit from positive content security rules that are maintained and enforced through continuous monitoring.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;detecting-malicious-intent-javascripts\&quot;&gt;Detecting malicious intent JavaScripts&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#detecting-malicious-intent-javascripts\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Managing client-side security is a massive data problem. For an average enterprise zone, our systems observe approximately 2,200 unique scripts; smaller business zones frequently handle around 1,000. This volume alone is difficult to manage, but the real challenge is the volatility of the code.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Roughly a third of these scripts undergo code updates within any 30-day window. If a security team attempted to manually approve every new DOM (document object model) interaction or outbound connection, the resulting overhead would paralyze the development pipeline.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Instead, our detection strategy focuses on &lt;i&gt;what a script is trying to do&lt;/i&gt;. That includes intent classification work &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-train-ai-to-uncover-malicious-javascript-intent-and-make-web-surfing-safer/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;weâve written about previously&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. In short, we analyze the script&amp;#39;s behavior using an Abstract Syntax Tree (AST). By breaking the code down into its logical structure, we can identify patterns that signal malicious intent, regardless of how the code is obfuscated.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-high-cost-of-false-positives\&quot;&gt;The high cost of false positives&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-high-cost-of-false-positives\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Client-side security operates differently than active vulnerability scanners deployed across the web, where a Web Application Firewall (WAF) would constantly observe matched attack signatures. While a WAF constantly blocks high-volume automated attacks, a client-side compromise (such as a breach of an origin server or a third-party vendor) is a rare, high-impact event. In an enterprise environment with rigorous vendor reviews and code scanning, these attacks are rare.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This rarity creates a problem. Because real attacks are infrequent, a security systemâs detections are statistically more likely to be false positives. For a security team, these false alarms create fatigue and hide real threats. To solve this, we integrated a Large Language Model (LLM) into our detection pipeline, drastically reducing the false positive rate.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;adding-an-llm-based-second-opinion-for-triage\&quot;&gt;Adding an LLM-based second opinion for triage&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#adding-an-llm-based-second-opinion-for-triage\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-train-ai-to-uncover-malicious-javascript-intent-and-make-web-surfing-safer/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;frontline detection engine&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; is a Graph Neural Network (GNN). GNNs are particularly well-suited for this task: they operate on the Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) of the JavaScript code, learning structural representations that capture execution patterns regardless of variable renaming, minification, or obfuscation. In machine learning terms, the GNN learns an embedding of the codeâs graph structure that generalizes across syntactic variations of the same semantic behavior.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The GNN is tuned for high recall. We want to catch novel, zero-day threats. Its precision is already remarkably high: less than 0.3% of total analyzed traffic is flagged as a false positive (FP). However, at Cloudflareâs scale of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-cloudflares-client-side-security-made-the-npm-supply-chain-attack-a-non/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;3.5 billion scripts assessed daily&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, even a sub-0.3% FP rate translates to a volume of false alarms that can be disruptive to customers.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The core issue is a classic class imbalance problem. While we can collect extensive malicious samples, the sheer diversity of benign JavaScript across the web is practically infinite. Heavily obfuscated but perfectly legitimate scripts â like bot challenges, tracking pixels, ad-tech bundles, and minified framework builds â can exhibit structural patterns that overlap with malicious code in the GNNâs learned feature space. As much as we try to cover a huge variety of interesting benign cases, the model simply has not seen enough of this infinite variety during training.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is precisely where Large Language Models (LLMs) complement the GNN. LLMs possess a deep semantic understanding of real-world JavaScript practices: they recognize domain-specific idioms, common framework patterns, and can distinguish sketchy-but-innocuous obfuscation from genuinely malicious intent.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Rather than replacing the GNN, we designed a cascading classifier architecture:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Every script is first evaluated by the GNN&lt;/b&gt;. If the GNN predicts the script as benign, the detection pipeline terminates immediately. &lt;b&gt;This incurs only the minimal latency of the GNN for the vast majority of traffic, completely bypassing the heavier computation time of the LLM&lt;/b&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;If the GNN flags the script as potentially malicious (above the decision threshold), the script is &lt;b&gt;forwarded to an open-source LLM&lt;/b&gt; hosted on Cloudflare &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers-ai/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workers AI&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for a second opinion.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The LLM, provided with a security-specialized prompt context, &lt;b&gt;semantically evaluates the scriptâs intent&lt;/b&gt;. If it determines the script is benign, it overrides the GNNâs verdict.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;This two-stage design gives us the best of both worlds: the GNNâs high recall for structural malicious patterns, combined with the LLMâs broad semantic understanding to filter out false positives.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/438frLuYPU51j0uhtM5foj/10c53b3b3ccc84b00c754c872ad20492/image3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1125\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-train-ai-to-uncover-malicious-javascript-intent-and-make-web-surfing-safer/#training-the-model-to-detect-hidden-malicious-intent\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;As we previously explained&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, our GNN is trained on publicly accessible script URLs, the same scripts any browser would fetch. The LLM inference at runtime runs entirely within Cloudflareâs network via &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers-ai/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workers AI&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; using open-source models (we currently use &lt;code&gt;gpt-oss-120b&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;As an additional safety net, every script flagged by the GNN is logged to Cloudflare &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/r2/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;R2&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for posterior analysis. This allows us to continuously audit whether the LLMâs overrides are correct and catch any edge cases where a true attack might have been inadvertently filtered out. Yes, we dogfood our own storage products for our own ML pipeline.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The results from our internal evaluations on real production traffic are compelling. Focusing on total analyzed traffic under the JS Integrity threat category, the secondary LLM validation layer reduced false positives by nearly 3x: dropping the already low ~0.3% FP rate down to ~0.1%. When evaluating unique scripts, the impact is even more dramatic: the FP rate plummets a whopping ~200x, from ~1.39% down to just 0.007%.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;At our scale, cutting the overall false positive rate by two-thirds translates to millions fewer false alarms for our customers every single day. Crucially, our True Positive (actual attack) detection capability includes a fallback mechanism:as noted above, we audit the LLMâs overrides to check for possible true attacks that were filtered by the LLM.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Because the LLM acts as a highly reliable precision filter in this pipeline, we can now afford to lower the GNNâs decision threshold, making it even more aggressive. This means we catch novel, highly obfuscated True Attacks that would have previously fallen just below the detection boundary, all without overwhelming customers with false alarms. In the next phase, we plan to push this even further.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;catching-zero-days-in-the-wild-the-core-js-router-exploit\&quot;&gt;Catching zero-days in the wild: The &lt;code&gt;core.js&lt;/code&gt; router exploit&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#catching-zero-days-in-the-wild-the-core-js-router-exploit\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;This two-stage architecture is already proving its worth in the wild. Just recently, our detection pipeline flagged a novel, highly obfuscated malicious script (&lt;code&gt;core.js&lt;/code&gt;) targeting users in specific regions.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In this case, the payload was engineered to commandeer home routers (specifically Xiaomi OpenWrt-based devices). Upon closer inspection via deobfuscation, the script demonstrated significant situational awareness: it queries the router&amp;#39;s WAN configuration (dynamically adapting its payload using parameters like &lt;code&gt;wanType=dhcp&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;wanType=static&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;wanType=pppoe&lt;/code&gt;), overwrites the DNS settings to hijack traffic through Chinese public DNS servers, and even attempts to lock out the legitimate owner by silently changing the admin password. Instead of compromising a website directly, it had been injected into users&amp;#39; sessions via compromised browser extensions.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To evade detection, the script&amp;#39;s core logic was heavily minified and packed using an array string obfuscator â a classic trick, but effective enough that traditional threat intelligence platforms like VirusTotal have not yet reported detections at the time of this writing.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Our GNN successfully revealed&lt;/b&gt; the underlying malicious structure despite the obfuscation, and the &lt;b&gt;Workers AI LLM confidently confirmed&lt;/b&gt; the intent. Here is a glimpse of the payload showing the target router API and the attempt to inject a rogue DNS server:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code
